Now let\'s see what the inspection stamp tells us.\\r\\n237: >\\r\\n238: > These sentences use different words, but they describe the same relationship. Let\'s write that relationship once.\\r\\n239: >\\r\\n240: > The arrow is pointing the right way. Check one more thing: did we keep the whole condition?\\r\\n241: \\r\\n242: Do not fill the gap with a curriculum announcement such as “Next, we will explore necessary conditions.” Say why the requirement is the next thing worth examining.\\r\\n243: \\r\\n244: ## Repetition changes the reader\'s view\\r\\n245: \\r\\n246: The breathing/air anchor appears in the opening, indicator families, contrapositive, fact chart, diagram, and target analysis. The reader keeps the same relationship while the representation changes. That saves the effort of understanding a new scenario at every turn.\\r\\n247: \\r\\n248: Within the contrapositive explanation, the source repeats the requirement in several forms: air is needed; breathing has to have air; without air the requirement is missing; the original condition cannot hold. Each repetition supplies a step or a new angle.\\r\\n249: \\r\\n250: For our writing, reuse an anchor while doing different work:\\r\\n251: \\r\\n252: 1. Read the ordinary meaning.\\r\\n253: 2. Apply it to a case.\\r\\n254: 3. Identify the sufficient and necessary ideas.\\r\\n255: 4. Compare an unsupported reverse.\\r\\n256: 5. Reword the same relationship.\\r\\n257: 6. Show the diagram after its meaning is understood.\\r\\n258: \\r\\n259: Introduce a fresh example for independent practice. The learner should not be able to answer only because the lesson already solved that sentence.\\r\\n260: \\r\\n261: A repeated “Keep this” slogan without new reasoning is not explanatory repetition. Nor is rendering the same instructions twice in different panels. See the demonstrated corrections in [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).\\r\\n262: \\r\\n263: ## Metaphor gives the reader a usable handle\\r\\n264: \\r\\n265: The source uses doors, families, activation, burdens, brackets, and putting yourself in a condition\'s shoes. Each metaphor gives an abstract operation a physical or social handle. A small set of terms recurs, so the reader can keep using it.\\r\\n266: \\r\\n267: Transfer the method of choosing a metaphor, not the particular inventory. Prefer a familiar image that helps answer a logical question. Explain its literal meaning immediately. Remove it when it adds more things to remember than it clarifies.\\r\\n268: \\r\\n269: For instance, a ticket can illustrate a requirement only if the rule is stated carefully. “A ticket is required to enter” does not say a ticket guarantees entry. An analogy fails if it smuggles in that reverse.\\r\\n270: \\r\\n271: Do not build a competing metaphor for every sentence. Do not invent branded names for every skill. The standard technical terms remain the stable vocabulary.\\r\\n272: \\r\\n273: ## Humor reduces tension without changing the rule\\r\\n274: \\r\\n275: The source\'s coffee obsession makes an unusual absolute rule memorable. Its reluctant-student aside acknowledges the experience of being asked to guess. The imagined green checkmark gives the reader\'s desire for certainty a recognizable shape.\\r\\n276: \\r\\n277: Humor can occur inside an explanation when it makes the thought easier to understand. Joshua\'s source selection supersedes the earlier blanket restriction that wit must stay outside the explanation. It does not authorize mockery, forced slang, exaggerated praise, or an expanding comic story.\\r\\n278: \\r\\n279: Use a light aside only when it helps the reader inhabit a situation or relax around a specific mistake. Keep it optional. A paragraph should still teach clearly if the aside is removed. Do not use the source\'s distinctive jokes as templates to be copied.\\r\\n280: \\r\\n281: Never turn first-person source anecdotes into Joshua\'s biography. “I ask every student this” requires that Joshua actually does. “Students always learn better this way” requires evidence or a narrower, truthful statement.\\r\\n282: \\r\\n283: ## Emphasis and visuals identify the decision\\r\\n284: \\r\\n285: Bold in the source often marks the rule, an inference, a warning, or an instruction. Italics distinguish examples and sometimes emphasize a word that changes the contrast. A table lets the reader compare controlled variations. A STOP asks the reader to attempt something. A side note answers a local question without interrupting the main line for everyone.\\r\\n286: \\r\\n287: For our material:\\r\\n288: \\r\\n289: - Bold the relationship or phrase that controls the next decision. Do not bold most of a paragraph.\\r\\n290: - Give an example its own space. The student should be able to find the sentence currently under discussion.\\r\\n291: - Put comparisons side by side when the same rule or wording is being contrasted.\\r\\n292: - Reveal diagram construction one meaningful step at a time. Explain each placement in English.\\r\\n293: - Preserve a readable text equivalent for every visual. The illustration is an explanation, not a substitute for one.\\r\\n294: - Make an interactive pause real. Let the learner choose when to reveal the reasoning.\\r\\n295: - Give a side note a specific job: answer a question, define new notation, or qualify advice. Avoid a second full lesson in a callout.\\r\\n296: \\r\\n297: Joshua\'s notation correction still applies: teaching arrows should look like teaching notation, not little implementation-code boxes. Clear ordinary typography and meaningful labels should communicate the relationship.\\r\\n298: \\r\\n299: The source\'s adjusted HTML design is not a visual brand template. Our bootcamp should preserve the established Intro to LR student flow while using original visuals that serve the current concept. Student pages show the assigned work and the lesson itself; internal stage planning, assigned-start metadata, production notes, and selectable difficulty controls do not belong in the teaching prose.\\r\\n300: \\r\\n301: ## The complete source progression and depth\\r\\n302: \\r\\n303: This map records what the supplied excerpt does in order. It is not an assertion that every future lesson should cover every listed topic.\\r\\n304: \\r\\n305: ### 1. The relationship comes before its parts\\r\\n306: \\r\\n307: **The Art of the If/Then**, HTML lines 142 to 154, opens with a plain definition, a single familiar rule, a literal interpretation, several statements of certainty, and a reason to understand the relationship beneath varied language. The transition promises to take the same example apart.\\r\\n308: \\r\\n309: The depth comes from making “must” meaningful before presenting a list of words. The reader learns what kind of relationship they are looking for. The source also connects certainty to prior “Power Players” material, which is not included in this attachment. Our standalone introduction must supply any needed context itself.\\r\\n310: \\r\\n311: **Transfer:** first explain what a conditional says and why its direction matters. Then give a concrete example. Do not open with a diagramming task or make the definition a label attached after a quiz.\\r\\n312: \\r\\n313: ### 2. Sufficient is explained through presence and absence\\r\\n314: \\r\\n315: **The Sufficient Condition**, lines 156 to 199, contains a definition, the recurring anchor, a metaphor, the possibility that the sufficient condition does not occur, a contrast between its absence and presence, and a clarification of “activation.” It then shows **eight indicator rows with eleven displayed example forms**, all expressing the same anchor relationship, and explains why language recognition cannot replace understanding.\\r\\n316: \\r\\n317: This is more than “sufficient goes left.” The text develops what the condition does, what it does not establish, and how wording changes around it.\\r\\n318: \\r\\n319: **Transfer:** give sufficient its own explanation, contrasting cases, and nearby practice. A left-side label is useful only after “enough for what?” has a clear answer.\\r\\n320: \\r\\n321: ### 3. Necessary is developed as a requirement\\r\\n322: \\r\\n323: **The Necessary Condition**, lines 201 to 230, defines the other part, reuses the same rule, explains the requirement, and shows **ten indicator rows with eleven displayed forms**. It compares the varied wording with the sufficient-family statements and gives special attention to “only if.” It ends by explaining why symbols help isolate the relationship.\\r\\n324: \\r\\n325: The source uses the ordinary force of “necessary,” “required,” and “need” to make the technical term familiar. It does not jump directly to a memorized side rule.\\r\\n326: \\r\\n327: **Transfer:** explain what is required and why having a requirement is not the same as satisfying every condition for the result. Teach “if” and “only if” using a controlled comparison. Keep the target of each expression clear.\\r\\n328: \\r\\n329: ### 4. The contrapositive is discovered before it is manipulated\\r\\n330: \\r\\n331: **The Contrapositive**, lines 234 to 285, first recovers the original relationship. It introduces a new coffee/walking thought experiment, removes the required condition, asks for a prediction, and explicitly pauses. The answer is derived from the unmet requirement. The term is then named, the original anchor revisited, and a four-situation chart sets limits on inference.\\r\\n332: \\r\\n333: The order is important: meaning, prediction, reason, name, comparison. The flip-and-negate shortcut appears later, after the reader has a reason to believe the result.\\r\\n334: \\r\\n335: **Transfer:** preserve this order when contraposition is actually in scope. Do not rush the full procedure into the opening definition merely because it appears early in this source.\\r\\n336: \\r\\n337: ### 5. Notation is introduced as a solution to varied wording\\r\\n338: \\r\\n339: **How to Diagram Conditionals**, lines 289 to 388, recovers the indicator families, explains the arrow, gives a four-step construction procedure, and builds one diagram in stages. It then explains negation, displays four original/contrapositive sign patterns, gives the shortcut, and distinguishes writing a contrapositive from reading one when needed.\\r\\n340: \\r\\n341: The first diagram is narrated: notice the indicator, place its target, then complete the other side. Its final appearance is not used as evidence that the learner understands how to make it.\\r\\n342: \\r\\n343: **Transfer:** show each operation and its reason. Preserve Joshua\'s method and meaningful labels rather than copying the source\'s four-step count or abbreviation rule.\\r\\n344: \\r\\n345: ### 6. Practical objections receive substantial answers\\r\\n346: \\r\\n347: **Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ**, lines 390 to 433, has five questions:\\r\\n348: \\r\\n349: | Reader question | Depth of the answer | Reusable teaching value |\\r\\n350: |---|---|---|\\r\\n351: | When should I diagram? | Three paragraphs distinguish early training from later judgment, discuss difficult wording and chains, and address avoidance caused by uncertainty. | Give a criterion, a reason, and an honest account of the learning process. |\\r\\n352: | How do I know the diagram is correct? | Three paragraphs connect confidence with checked work and studying specific errors. | Teach a verification habit instead of offering confidence as a substitute for one. |\\r\\n353: | Does diagramming take too long? | Two paragraphs explain the proposed tradeoff and separate learning from speed. | Acknowledge the concern; explain why the slower practice step is useful now. |\\r\\n354: | Should I always write the contrapositive? | Two paragraphs qualify the advice. A separate note explains why the drills still require it. | Distinguish training evidence from the eventual performance habit. |\\r\\n355: | How should I abbreviate? | Gives a short example, a complete diagram, and advice about compact labels. | Make the notation choice purposeful and readable. Joshua\'s label guidance governs where it differs. |\\r\\n356: \\r\\n357: This is unusually important to the overall tone. The writer gives practical uncertainty enough room to be answered. It is not squeezed into one generic reassurance.\\r\\n358: \\r\\n359: ### 7. A useful shortcut is shown, then limited\\r\\n360: \\r\\n361: **Figuring Out The Language**, lines 437 to 471, defines an indicator\'s target and brackets the target in familiar sentences. It first shows the common placement immediately after the indicator. Then it gives five examples in which the target comes before the indicator, followed by three paired wording variations showing that the same indicator can switch position.\\r\\n362: \\r\\n363: The sequence deliberately prevents an attractive mechanical generalization. The reader sees why adjacency often helps and why it cannot replace reading the sentence.\\r\\n364: \\r\\n365: **Transfer:** teach the indicator\'s complete target. Change clause order while holding meaning steady. Include one placement trap only after the usual case is understood. Mark the obscured final line as missing evidence.\\r\\n366: \\r\\n367: ### 8. A translation is checked by trying both interpretations\\r\\n368: \\r\\n369: **Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then**, lines 475 to 508, defines the check, explains its purpose, gives four steps, establishes the ordinary meaning of a new sentence, and works through a correct guess. It then deliberately tries the reversed guess and explains why that meaning fails.\\r\\n370: \\r\\n371: This adds a verification strategy after notation. It also models the experience of being uncertain without abandoning the task. The teacher does not merely say “use your intuition”; the reader sees how to test an interpretation.\\r\\n372: \\r\\n373: **Transfer:** use the imagined situation as a meaning check. Preserve who or what the sentence concerns. Personalization is optional if pretending to be an object makes the wording less natural.\\r\\n374: \\r\\n375: ### 9. Practice changes the language while keeping the skill\\r\\n376: \\r\\n377: **Basic Conditional Drill**, lines 512 to 563, gives supportive instructions and six themed items, with original and contrapositive diagrams displayed in the supplied HTML. The task is the same across varied sentence forms. The source says the adapted sentences come from Shakespeare and points to an answer key not included here.\\r\\n378: \\r\\n379: **Transfer:** original practice can have a memorable theme, but it should change only what the learner is ready to handle. Reuse the skill, not the source\'s exercise wording. Show answers after an attempt and explain why each diagram preserves the complete ideas. Do not treat the displayed annotations as an independently verified answer key.\\r\\n380: \\r\\n381: ## What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp\\r\\n382: \\r\\n383: The immediate authoring scope remains **what conditionals are, why they matter, sufficient, necessary, Group 1, and Group 2**. The detailed source is a quality reference. Its later topics do not automatically enter the opening.\\r\\n384: \\r\\n385: | Part of our opening | What the learner should understand | Natural teaching progression | Nearby practice |\\r\\n386: |---|---|---|---|\\r\\n387: | **What a conditional says** | A statement makes one condition enough to require another. It does not establish that either condition occurs. | Plain definition, purpose, one stated rule, one case, one tempting reverse. | Decide what the given rule and fact establish; explain the limit. |\\r\\n388: | **Why it matters on the LSAT** | A conclusion can rely on the direction of a rule or on a missing connection. Reversing a relationship can support the wrong answer. | Keep the beginner example; show one supported conclusion and one unsupported inference. | Choose the justified conclusion and name the connection the other answer would require. |\\r\\n389: | **Sufficient** | Enough to establish the necessary condition under this rule; not the only possible way unless stated. | Same anchor, “enough for what?”, present-case explanation, absence leaves the other condition open. | Find the complete sufficient idea and say what it establishes. |\\r\\n390: | **Necessary** | Required for the sufficient condition; presence alone does not establish the sufficient condition. | Same anchor, identify what must be there, compare requirement with guarantee. | Identify the requirement; reject an unsupported reverse with a reason. |\\r\\n391: | **Translation and diagramming** | The diagram preserves the whole relationship, including conditions and qualifications. | Ordinary meaning, indicator, complete ideas, placement, English readback. Introduce later operations when their prerequisites are taught. | Construct a diagram in meaningful steps and compare it with the whole sentence. |\\r\\n392: | **Group 1** | Joshua\'s Group 1 expressions identify the sufficient idea; sentence order can vary. | Teach a simple expression first, reuse meaning across variants, then alter position and scope. | Lesson, guided translation, fresh independent items, explained feedback. |\\r\\n393: | **Group 2** | Joshua\'s Group 2 expressions identify the necessary idea; target and force must be read in context. | Teach the requirement first, compare “if” with “only if,” then develop each expression\'s target and nuances. | Separate Group 2 practice, then a controlled Group 1/2 comparison when both are learned. |\\r\\n394: \\r\\n395: These are curriculum planning rows. Student-facing headings should teach the actual concept, such as **What does this sentence require?** They should not expose “Joshua\'s assigned starting stage,” internal sequencing metadata, or a difficulty selector. Joshua assigns the appropriate material; the learner works through that assigned lesson and its practice.\\r\\n396: \\r\\n397: ### Give an idea several teachable moments\\r\\n398: \\r\\n399: The source rarely handles a significant concept with one definition and one answer. It may need a literal explanation, a familiar example, a variation, a pause, a mistaken interpretation, a correction, and a short practice task.\\r\\n400: \\r\\n401: For Group 1, that could become several small lessons: recognizing “if,” locating the complete if-clause, reading an if-clause at the end of the sentence, handling a universal expression, and checking that the diagram preserves the meaning. Group 2 deserves its own development. Do not flatten all expressions into a word table and assume coverage.\\r\\n402: \\r\\n403: Keep micro-assignments possible. A student who needs “only if” should be able to receive that work without repeating every beginner concept. A general assignment can connect the lessons into a full sequence. Beginner, medium, and advanced describe teacher-assigned work; optional timing belongs to medium and advanced practice after accuracy is established.\\r\\n404: \\r\\n405: ### Preserve the real indicator groups\\r\\n406: \\r\\n407: The current reviewed opening materials use:\\r\\n408: \\r\\n409: - **Group 1:** if, when, where, all, the only, every, any.\\r\\n410: - **Group 2:** only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.\\r\\n411: \\r\\n412: See the [reviewed opening source constraints](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/sufficient-necessary-infographic-prompt.txt). These lists are current project teaching groups, not a claim that every occurrence of each word mechanically diagrammed in isolation has the same function. The supplied source\'s two families have different members. Do not merge inventories without checking Joshua\'s notes and scope.\\r\\n413: \\r\\n414: “The only” especially requires its complete grammatic…8045 tokens truncated…statement, and then say, “We know the necessary condition is happening!” When they do this, you should be like, “Cool. So I can ignore the conditional.” <strong>The presence of the necessary condition doesn’t make anything happen.</strong></p>\\n\\n<p>Check out this chart showing what does and <em>does not</em> activate your conditional relationship:</p>\\n\\n<table>\\n  <thead>\\n    <tr><th>Situations</th><th>What Has to Happen?</th><th>If I’m Breathing, Then Air Is Present.</th></tr>\\n  </thead>\\n  <tbody>\\n    <tr><td><strong>Sufficient Present</strong></td><td><em>Must have necessary condition</em></td><td><em>I’m breathing, so there must be air.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><strong>Necessary Absent</strong></td><td><em>Can’t have sufficient condition</em></td><td><em>There’s no air, so I can’t breathe.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><strong>Sufficient Absent</strong></td><td><em>Nothing!</em></td><td><em>There’s no breathing happening, so we don’t know anything else.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><strong>Necessary Present</strong></td><td><em>Nothing!</em></td><td><em>There’s air around, so we don’t know anything else.</em></td></tr>\\n  </tbody>\\n</table>\\n\\n<p>The first two rows of this chart represent the only two situations that make anything happen with your conditional relationship. The conditional is just a rule; it takes the right situation to activate it. Literally anything other than those two things can happen and you don’t need to do anything with your conditional. <strong>Without the situations in the first two rows of this chart, the conditional might as well not exist.</strong></p>\\n\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\n\\n<h2>How to Diagram Conditionals</h2>\\n\\n<p>Now that we have a handle on the Must Relationship, let’s take a look at how to represent it symbolically.</p>\\n\\n<table>\\n  <thead><tr><th>IF — Sufficient Condition</th><th>→</th><th>THEN — Necessary Condition</th></tr></thead>\\n  <tbody>\\n    <tr>\\n      <td>\\n        when(ever)<br>\\n        any(time)<br>\\n        all<br>\\n        every(time)<br>\\n        in order to<br>\\n        people who<br>\\n        each\\n      </td>\\n      <td>→</td>\\n      <td>\\n        must<br>\\n        necessary<br>\\n        required<br>\\n        only (if)<br>\\n        depends<br>\\n        need (to)<br>\\n        have to<br>\\n        essential<br>\\n        precondition\\n      </td>\\n    </tr>\\n  </tbody>\\n</table>\\n\\n<h3>The Must Arrow</h3>\\n\\n<p>The conditional relationship is ideal for symbolic representation because it is always the same underneath the varying language. We symbolize the conditional relationship with one simple sign:</p>\\n\\n<div class=\\"diagram\\">Sufficient  →  Necessary\\n              ↑\\n          Must Arrow</div>\\n\\n<p>We use the Must Arrow to signify the Must Relationship. It is a reminder of the urgency of conditionals. Every conditional relationship can be written like this. We call it <em>diagramming the conditional</em>.</p>\\n\\n<p>Here are your steps for diagramming a conditional relationship:</p>\\n\\n<ol>\\n  <li>See a conditional indicator.</li>\\n  <li>Draw a Must Arrow.</li>\\n  <li>Place an abbreviation of the word or phrase targeted by the conditional indicator on the correct side of the Must Arrow.\\n    <ul>\\n      <li>Sufficient is on the left, before the Must Arrow.</li>\\n      <li>Necessary is on the right, after the Must Arrow.</li>\\n    </ul>\\n  </li>\\n  <li>Fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with whatever is left over in the statement.</li>\\n</ol>\\n\\n<p>Let’s take diagramming conditionals for a spin together. We’ll start with our favorite statement:</p>\\n\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\n\\n<p>I see a sufficient indicator in front of breathing, so I’ll place a <strong>B</strong> in the sufficient condition:</p>\\n\\n<div class=\\"diagram\\">B →</div>\\n\\n<p>Now, I’ll fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with <strong>A</strong> for air, since that’s what’s left in the statement.</p>\\n\\n<div class=\\"diagram\\">B → A</div>\\n\\n<p>There you go. Your first fully diagrammed conditional! Now let’s talk through the contrapositive. If the necessary condition is gone, we know the sufficient is gone as well. We can diagram the contrapositive as:</p>\\n\\n<div class=\\"diagram\\">~A → ~B</div>\\n\\n<div class=\\"note\\">Check out the little squiggle (~) in front of the ~A and ~B. We’ll use the squiggle throughout this text to negate variables. Read the squiggle as a “not.”</div>\\n\\n<p>Check out a few more contrapositive examples:</p>\\n\\n<table>\\n  <thead><tr><th>Original Statement</th><th>Equivalent Contrapositive</th></tr></thead>\\n  <tbody>\\n    <tr><td>A → B</td><td>~B → ~A</td></tr>\\n    <tr><td>Q → ~R</td><td>R → ~Q</td></tr>\\n    <tr><td>~L → P</td><td>~P → L</td></tr>\\n    <tr><td>~Y → ~X</td><td>X → Y</td></tr>\\n  </tbody>\\n</table>\\n\\n<p><strong>Once you have the original statement written down, you can flip and negate both sides to quickly jot down the contrapositive.</strong></p>\\n\\n<p><strong>I don’t recommend diagramming the contrapositive on every conditional you encounter</strong> just for the sake of doing so, but sometimes it will be useful to write the conditional in its contrapositive form, either for clarity or to facilitate a chain. <strong>Conditionals can be written either way: as the original statement or as the contrapositive.</strong> Both statements are describing the same relationship.</p>\\n\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">\\n  <p>Instead of writing the contrapositive out every time, you can just read the contrapositive when it’s relevant. Every conditional can be read two ways:</p>\\n  <ol>\\n    <li>Read it forwards from left to right.</li>\\n    <li>Read it backwards from right to left, negating everything as you go (reading the contrapositive without having to write it out).</li>\\n  </ol>\\n</div>\\n\\n<p>Awesome! Let’s talk through some frequent diagramming issues together.</p>\\n\\n<h3>Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ</h3>\\n\\n<div class=\\"qa\\">\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>When should I diagram a conditional?</em></div>\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\n    <p><strong>In the beginning, you should always diagram conditionals.</strong> This trains you to make sure you’re actually thinking conditionally. The language in LR is your biggest obstacle. Diagramming the conditional gets rid of all the linguistic ambiguity. You don’t have to look back at the text once you’re sure you diagrammed the conditional correctly. (I’ll get to how you’ll be sure in a second. Hold tight.)</p>\\n    <p>When you are more experienced, you should diagram whenever the stimulus is not easy. I always err on the side of diagramming; I think of it as using the paper to my advantage. If I can take down a note to free myself of the exact words of the stimulus, I’m going to do it. I see far more problems from students declining to diagram than I do from students who diagram too much. If there is more than one conditional in a stimulus, you should diagram them to see if there is chaining potential.</p>\\n    <p>Besides timing concerns, I mostly see people decline to diagram because they aren’t confident that their diagram will be correct, and they feel safer sticking to the exact words of the stimulus because then they “can’t screw it up.” But you need to fail a couple times in order to build the skill. That’s how you get better. Don’t give up before you start. This leads me to the next question.</p>\\n  </div>\\n\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>How will I ever be sure my conditional is correctly diagrammed?</em></div>\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\n    <p>Seeing it work out correctly enough times breeds confidence. In your Wrong Answer Journal, keep track of every time you incorrectly diagram a conditional and make note of exactly how the language tricked you. Study these errors so you don’t make the same mistake again. <strong>Your mistakes are your greatest asset.</strong></p>\\n    <p>The only way to ever be 100% sure that your diagram is right is confidence in your own abilities. There will never be a little green checkmark that spontaneously appears on the page to tell you that you’re right.</p>\\n    <p>Everyone messes this up in the beginning. You can’t let that kill your confidence. Do the work, build skill, and you will eventually know that you’re right.</p>\\n  </div>\\n\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>But diagramming takes sooooo much time. I need to be fast. Do I really have to?</em></div>\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\n    <p>Diagramming does not take nearly as much time as you imagine it does. There is a huge gap between perception and reality when it comes to LR timing. It takes a lot less time to diagram than it does to reread a difficult sentence ad infinitum. Writing things down frees up mental resources for other tasks. You need all the mental power you can get when you’re doing LR, so use the paper to your advantage.</p>\\n    <p>Also, you have to walk before you can run. You’re not going to be fast at first and that’s ok. This book contains strategies designed to help you get faster, but you’re not there yet, and attempting to get there now will only delay your progress toward mastering these concepts.</p>\\n  </div>\\n\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>Should I always diagram the contrapositive?</em></div>\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\n    <p>No. Many people claim that always diagramming the contrapositive saves time, but it actually ends up costing you much more time than it saves, especially on Logic Games. You should understand conditional reasoning on a deep enough level to look at the original conditional diagram and know that the contrapositive is also true (reading the contrapositive instead of writing it). This works for the same reason you knew the contrapositive was true in our initial contrapositive thought experiment. The contrapositive is not a separate statement. It is contained within the original statement. If you can write it, you can already read it, so don’t waste time putting extra noise on the paper.</p>\\n    <p>That said, there are times when diagramming the contrapositive is useful, and you should definitely diagram it if you have a reason to do so. Sometimes you’ll need the contrapositive to facilitate a chain. Just don’t write the contrapositive out of habit and you’ll do great.</p>\\n  </div>\\n</div>\\n\\n<div class=\\"note\\">You’ll diagram the contrapositive in all the drills in this chapter. Why? I want to verify that you have the ability to identify the contrapositive. Once you see that your contrapositives match the drill answer keys, you’ll be more confident simply reading the contrapositive in your head from the original statement.</div>\\n\\n<div class=\\"qa\\">\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">Q.</div><div><em>What’s this abbreviation stuff?</em></div>\\n  <div class=\\"label\\">A.</div><div>\\n    <p>When you diagram, you should shorten each condition to a capital letter or two. For example:</p>\\n    <p class=\\"example\\">If I’m a chair, then I’m furniture.</p>\\n    <p>We would shorten the “chair” to C and shorten the “furniture” to F. Ending up with this diagram:</p>\\n    <div class=\\"diagram\\">C → F</div>\\n    <p>For more complex conditions, just use the minimum number of letters that you will actually understand. Say one of the conditions is “maximum return on investment,” you should shorten that to MRI or MR… Whichever option calls up the condition in your mind will work. <strong>Don’t write out the whole word or more than four letters per condition.</strong> The notation becomes cumbersome when you write more than that.</p>\\n  </div>\\n</div>\\n\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\n\\n<h2>Figuring Out The Language</h2>\\n\\n<p>Let’s tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning: figuring out the language. We’ll start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word.</p>\\n\\n<p><strong>The indicator’s target is the word or phrase it is referring to.</strong></p>\\n\\n<p>In this list, I put the conditional indicator’s target in a little bracket cage.</p>\\n\\n<table>\\n  <tbody>\\n    <tr><td><em>If [I’m breathing], then air is present.</em></td><td><em>Each [instance of breathing] has air.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><em>When [I’m breathing], air is present.</em></td><td><em>I’m breathing, only if [air is present].</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><em>Any [breathing] means air is present.</em></td><td><em>Only [the presence of air] allows for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><em>All [instances of breathing] have air present.</em></td><td><em>Breathing depends on [air being present].</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><em>Every [instance of breathing] has air present.</em></td><td><em>I need [air] to breathe.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><em>In order to [breathe], I must have air.</em></td><td><em>I have to have [air], if I’m breathing.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><em>People who [breathe] need air.</em></td><td></td></tr>\\n  </tbody>\\n</table>\\n\\n<p>Notice that in each of these examples, the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read, but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. This generalization isn’t always true:</p>\\n\\n<table>\\n  <tbody>\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] must be present for breathing.</em></td><td><em>[Air] is essential for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] is necessary for breathing.</em></td><td><em>[Air] is a precondition for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] is required for breathing.</em></td><td></td></tr>\\n  </tbody>\\n</table>\\n\\n<p>In these examples, the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult, you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is <strong>really saying</strong>. They don’t want you to see an indicator word and autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Many of them can switch sides. Look at the variations on a few of these conditionals:</p>\\n\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] must be present for breathing. / There must be [air] for us to breathe.</p>\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] is necessary for breathing. / Breathing makes it necessary to have [air].</p>\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] is required for breathing. / I require [air] to breathe.</p>\\n\\n<p>[Final line at the bottom of the image is partially obscured.]</p>\\n\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\n\\n<h2>Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then</h2>\\n\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">Personalized if/thens are translations for conditionals.</div>\\n\\n<p>Here’s another safeguard on conditional diagramming. <strong>Make a habit of translating conditionals into personalized if/thens.</strong></p>\\n\\n<p><strong>Personalized if/thens require you to put yourself in the conditional statement and ask yourself what you know about your situation.</strong> This can get you out of confusion panic and back to the real world.</p>\\n\\n<h3>How to Create a Personalized If/Then</h3>\\n\\n<p>Imagine you’re confused on what a conditional means. Instead of panicking:</p>\\n\\n<ol>\\n  <li>Try to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning of the sentence.</li>\\n  <li>Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition.</li>\\n  <li>Put yourself in your guess’ situation and ask what you know about yourself.</li>\\n  <li>If your guess is right, you will know that the necessary condition has to pertain.</li>\\n</ol>\\n\\n<p>Let’s translate an example into a personalized if/then:</p>\\n\\n<p class=\\"example\\">Orchids depend on frequent conversation.</p>\\n\\n<p>Before you proceed, think about what this sentence means for real. You need to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning before you can check your personalized if/then against that meaning. The sentence means orchids need frequent conversation. First we’ll guess that orchids is the sufficient condition and see if this fits with our casual meaning of the sentence:</p>\\n\\n<div class=\\"note\\">The key to intuitively understanding conditionals is personalizing them. Always put yourself in the shoes of the conditional, no matter what it’s about.</div>\\n\\n<p>So, if I’m an orchid, what do I know about me? I know that I have to have frequent conversation. I depend on frequent conversation. If I’m an orchid, <em>then</em> I have frequent conversation. This fits. It is the correct translation of the conditional.</p>\\n\\n<p>Now, let’s imagine that I guessed incorrectly and tried to see if frequent conversation could be the sufficient condition. The personalized if/then would help me catch my error:</p>\\n\\n<p>So, if I’m a frequent conversation, what do I know about me? If I’m a frequent conversation, what do I need? Do I need an orchid? Ehh… I don’t need anything, right? Orchids are the ones who need something in this sentence. If I’m frequent conversation, I don’t <em>need</em> anything else. This tells me that frequent conversation is not the correct sufficient condition. This is how you can detect that you’ve made a mistake. It is the incorrect translation of the conditional.</p>\\n\\n<p><strong>The personalized if/then is how I always do conditionals, unless they are already phrased as if/thens.</strong> It gives me confidence throughout the test that I’m not making a mistake.</p>\\n\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\n\\n<h2>Basic Conditional Drill — Much Ado About Nothing</h2>\\n\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">The conditional drills in this chapter are each inspired by Shakespeare plays, which you will see identified in the title of each drill. The lines are very lightly edited to highlight their conditionality, but these are more-or-less the real lines from Shakespeare. And yes, Shakespeare is really this outlandish; high school didn’t do him justice.</div>\\n\\n<h3>Instructions</h3>\\n\\n<p><em>Diagram the following conditional statements. Don’t be afraid to look back at the section, if you need any help.</em></p>\\n\\n<ol>\\n  <li>\\n    <p>If he were in my books, I’d burn my study.</p>\\n    <p><strong>Original</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">B → S</span></p>\\n    <p><strong>Contrapositive</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">~S → ~B</span></p>\\n  </li>\\n  <li>\\n    <p>Every time I hear my dog bark at a crow, it’s like hearing a man swear he loves me.</p>\\n    <p><strong>Original</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">DBC → CM</span></p>\\n    <p><strong>Contrapositive</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">~CM → ~DBC</span></p>\\n  </li>\\n  <li>\\n    <p>If I were a man, I would eat his heart in the marketplace.</p>\\n    <p><strong>Original</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">IM → EHM</span></p>\\n    <p><strong>Contrapositive</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">~EHM → ~IM</span></p>\\n  </li>\\n  <li>\\n    <p>I am heart-burned an hour after whenever I see him.</p>\\n    <p><strong>Original</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">SH → HB</span></p>\\n    <p><strong>Contrapositive</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">~HB → ~SH</span></p>\\n  </li>\\n  <li>\\n    <p>Benedick is always a rare parrot-teacher.</p>\\n    <p><strong>Original</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">B → RPT</span></p>\\n    <p><strong>Contrapositive</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">~RPT → ~B</span></p>\\n  </li>\\n  <li>\\n    <p>Love requires me to transform into an oyster.</p>\\n    <p><strong>Original</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">L → TO</span></p>\\n    <p><strong>Contrapositive</strong>: <span class=\\"handwritten\\">~TO → ~L</span></p>\\n  </li>\\n</ol>\\n\\n<div class=\\"note\\">\\n  <strong>Where’s the answer key?</strong>\\n  <p>You’ll find the answer key at the end of the chapter on page 148.</p>\\n</div>\\n\\n</main>\\n</body>\\n</html>\\n\\r\\n"}}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 3.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"68869d","wall_time_seconds":2.8485296,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4331,"output":"414: “The only” especially requires its complete grammatical target. The only people admitted are members means **Admitted → Member**. Only members are admitted means the same relationship, reached through a different expression. The lesson must show the meaning rather than encourage a next-word shortcut.\\r\\n415: \\r\\n416: ### Preserve Joshua\'s five steps\\r\\n417: \\r\\n418: In [Sandra\'s 2026-09-28 transcript](../students/sandra/transcript-downloads/2026-09-28-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md), at 00:43:22, Joshua gives this sequence: identify the indicator; label the two ideas; apply the left/right rule; find the contrapositive; say it back in English. This is a summary of his sequence, not a verbatim quotation. At 00:37:08, he stresses understanding and reading the diagram rather than retaining bare letters.\\r\\n419: \\r\\n420: The supplied source has four construction steps. Do not quietly replace Joshua\'s five with those four, or claim a shortened opening exercise has taught all five. In a gradual introduction, teach the operations whose prerequisites have been established and identify the full contrapositive step when that lesson is reached. Keep English readback visible as a meaning check throughout.\\r\\n421: \\r\\n422: ### Carry these improvements forward\\r\\n423: \\r\\n424: 1. **Define before demonstrating.** Start by explaining what a conditional says. Let the example make the definition concrete.\\r\\n425: 2. **Explain the purpose through a decision.** Show how direction changes the conclusion the learner may draw. Avoid unsupported prevalence statistics.\\r\\n426: 3. **Keep one relationship stable while changing the view.** Ordinary sentence, case fact, roles, wording variation, and diagram should fit together.\\r\\n427: 4. **Make each pause a useful thinking task.** Ask for a prediction or explanation, then reveal the reason.\\r\\n428: 5. **Show a mistake being detected.** A wrong arrow becomes understandable when its English meaning fails the original sentence.\\r\\n429: 6. **Teach the target before the placement shortcut becomes a habit.** Complete ideas and their qualifications matter.\\r\\n430: 7. **Explain why the drill exists.** The learner should know what ability they are practicing.\\r\\n431: 8. **Use fresh practice.** Worked examples teach the method; new items show whether the method transfers.\\r\\n432: 9. **Give practical objections an answer.** Time, confidence, notation, and when to write deserve specific guidance.\\r\\n433: 10. **Keep later content in its proper lesson.** Contraposition, chains, Groups 3/4, and quantities receive their own development; a quality reference does not override prerequisites.\\r\\n434: \\r\\n435: ## Accuracy and method boundaries\\r\\n436: \\r\\n437: Writing that sounds like the source must still preserve correct logic and Joshua\'s chosen method.\\r\\n438: \\r\\n439: | Source simplification or context | Boundary for our material |\\r\\n440: |---|---|\\r\\n441: | “Ignore the conditional” when sufficient is absent or necessary is present. | For this one rule and fact, no new conclusion about the other condition follows. The rule remains available for later facts and chains. Never teach that it disappears. |\\r\\n442: | Necessary has to happen “at some point.” | A conditional is not necessarily a time sequence or causal claim. Prefer “has to be true if the sufficient condition is true.” |\\r\\n443: | The original and contrapositive are “all you know.” | The central warning is against an unsupported reverse or inverse. Equivalent formulations and other supplied rules still matter. State the immediate inference limits precisely. |\\r\\n444: | Required words are described as obligations. | Preserve modality. “Interviewees must bring ID” can assert an obligation, not prove compliance. Diagram the stated requirement unless the facts establish compliance. |\\r\\n445: | Indicator usually comes before target. | Read its full grammatical target. Targets can precede the indicator, and familiar words can serve other roles in context. |\\r\\n446: | Shorten conditions to one or two letters, never more than four. | Joshua\'s clear condensed labels govern. Keep negation, scope, qualifiers, and readability. A short label that loses meaning is not an improvement. |\\r\\n447: | Try a personalized sufficient guess. | Compare with the whole ordinary meaning and look for a counterexample. A feeling that an arrow is plausible is not enough. |\\r\\n448: | Original and contrapositive training. | Explain why a drill requires explicit work and when test-day practice may differ. Do not turn a training requirement into a universal performance rule. |\\r\\n449: | “Always” is in a necessary-family teaching list. | Establish the sentence\'s subject and relationship. It is not a universal instruction to place the next word on the right. |\\r\\n450: | Confidence guarantees the diagram is correct. | Confidence can grow from practice, but correctness rests on preserving meaning and valid reasoning. Provide checks. |\\r\\n451: | Timing and learning claims are absolute. | Treat them as the author\'s advice unless independently supported. Give a practical explanation without invented evidence. |\\r\\n452: | References to Logic Games, a book, page 148, or named source tools. | Preserve the teaching function only where our course has an actual equivalent. Do not import obsolete or unavailable context. |\\r\\n453: | A theme makes a drill memorable. | Keep each sentence logically interpretable and its answer reviewed. Novelty must not obscure the condition or require untaught knowledge. |\\r\\n454: \\r\\n455: For the parcel example, three possible cases distinguish the permitted relationship from an invalid reverse:\\r\\n456: \\r\\n457: - Blue sticker and inspected: fits the rule.\\r\\n458: - Inspected without a blue sticker: also fits the rule.\\r\\n459: - Blue sticker without inspection: breaks the rule.\\r\\n460: \\r\\n461: The rule does not itself say any such parcel exists. Examples of possible cases illustrate consistency, not existence claims. This distinction matters when moving from individual facts to general rules.\\r\\n462: \\r\\n463: ## Calibration examples for different LSAT surfaces\\r\\n464: \\r\\n465: All examples below are original drafts for this reference. They demonstrate writing mechanisms; they are not assigned work or approved curriculum.\\r\\n466: \\r\\n467: ### Explain sufficient and necessary without rushing\\r\\n468: \\r\\n469: > Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.\\r\\n470: >\\r\\n471: > Suppose yours has a blue sticker. That\'s enough to know it has been inspected. You don\'t need another fact about the parcel. The rule has already told you what must be true of every parcel with that sticker.\\r\\n472: >\\r\\n473: > We call the blue sticker the **sufficient condition**. Sufficient means enough. Here, enough to establish inspection under this rule.\\r\\n474: >\\r\\n475: > Inspection is the **necessary condition**. A blue-sticker parcel has to meet that requirement. Otherwise it would be an exception to a rule that allows no exceptions.\\r\\n476: >\\r\\n477: > Now suppose a parcel has been inspected. Does it have to have a blue sticker?\\r\\n478: >\\r\\n479: > We still don\'t know. Inspection was required for the blue-sticker parcels. The shop never said those were the only parcels it inspected.\\r\\n480: \\r\\n481: This returns to meaning at every term. The example does not assert why the shop uses stickers or when they are applied. Those extra facts are unnecessary.\\r\\n482: \\r\\n483: ### Teach a necessary expression through its whole meaning\\r\\n484: \\r\\n485: > Editing this newsletter requires an account.\\r\\n486: >\\r\\n487: > What does the sentence say the editor needs? An account. Keep that complete idea on the required side of the relationship:\\r\\n488: >\\r\\n489: > **Edits this newsletter → Has an account**\\r\\n490: >\\r\\n491: > Read it back: if someone edits this newsletter, they have an account. That matches the sentence.\\r\\n492: >\\r\\n493: > Try the reverse. If someone has an account, do they necessarily edit the newsletter? The sentence hasn\'t told us that. An account could belong to someone who only reads it.\\r\\n494: \\r\\n495: The final possible counterexample is introduced as something consistent with the sentence, not an asserted fact about this system. Technical placement has a semantic reason.\\r\\n496: \\r\\n497: ### Give a flashcard a reasoning task\\r\\n498: \\r\\n499: **Front**\\r\\n500: \\r\\n501: > Every package in Locker 4 has been scanned. A package has been scanned. Must it be in Locker 4? Why?\\r\\n502: \\r\\n503: **Back**\\r\\n504: \\r\\n505: > No. The rule tells us about packages already in Locker 4. It doesn\'t say every scanned package goes there.\\r\\n506: \\r\\n507: This is more useful than a card that only asks “Necessary condition = which side?” Keep simple recall cards when recall is the actual skill; do not mistake that for complete reasoning practice.\\r\\n508: \\r\\n509: ### Make feedback identify the missed connection\\r\\n510: \\r\\n511: > You used the sentence in the reverse direction. “Only registered visitors enter the archive” requires registration for entry. It hasn\'t said every registered visitor enters. Read the diagram as **Enters → Registered** and compare it with the sentence.\\r\\n512: \\r\\n513: The feedback names the mistake, shows the relevant meaning, and gives a repair. It avoids generic praise, mockery, and labels such as “weak reasoning.”\\r\\n514: \\r\\n515: ### Explain an ordinary learning objection\\r\\n516: \\r\\n517: > “I understood the sentence. Why write it down?”\\r\\n518: >\\r\\n519: > With one short rule, you may not need to. When a passage gives you several relationships, a diagram lets you compare them without rereading the wording each time. In this exercise, write the relationship so we can check that you kept its direction and complete ideas.\\r\\n520: \\r\\n521: The answer gives a decision criterion and distinguishes the purpose of this exercise from a blanket rule about every test question.\\r\\n522: \\r\\n523: ### Transfer the voice to another LSAT topic\\r\\n524: \\r\\n525: > A conclusion is the claim the author is asking you to accept. The other statements are supposed to support it.\\r\\n526: >\\r\\n527: > Nora says, “The library should stay open later because every evening seat was taken this week.” What is she trying to persuade us of?\\r\\n528: >\\r\\n529: > That the library should stay open later. The full seats are her reason. She expects that reason to support the proposed change.\\r\\n530: >\\r\\n531: > You don\'t have to agree with her. First, separate what she wants you to accept from what she gives you in support.\\r\\n532: \\r\\n533: The same mechanism works outside conditionals: definition, small example, question, explanation, and a useful boundary. No conditional topic has been imported into the conclusion lesson.\\r\\n534: \\r\\n535: ## Revise the draft in a useful order\\r\\n536: \\r\\n537: ### First, check the reasoning and scope\\r\\n538: \\r\\n539: List what is given, what follows, and why. Try to construct a case that satisfies the given information but makes the proposed conclusion false. If that case is possible, revise the logic before polishing the voice. Check modality, negation, quantifier scope, and whether the learner has actually been taught every required operation.\\r\\n540: \\r\\n541: For a rewrite, preserve Joshua\'s established method and explicit lesson scope. The new reference governs delivery. It does not authorize removing content that students need or adding source topics the current assignment does not cover.\\r\\n542: \\r\\n543: ### Then, check the learner\'s experience\\r\\n544: \\r\\n545: Read as someone who has never seen the notation. Can they tell what the concept is? Can they answer the first question with what the page has taught? Does each new term refer to an idea they have seen? Does the page tell them why the correct answer follows?\\r\\n546: \\r\\n547: Put the explanation beside the point where it is needed. Do not rely on an interaction to imply a distinction the prose never explains. A student who cannot use the animation must still be able to learn from the text.\\r\\n548: \\r\\n549: ### Then, listen to the voice\\r\\n550: \\r\\n551: Read aloud at an ordinary teaching pace. Replace stiff phrasing with the words the teacher would naturally use. Keep the intermediate thoughts. Vary sentence length where the task changes. Remove repeated catchphrases, inflated enthusiasm, branded metaphors, and irrelevant asides.\\r\\n552: \\r\\n553: Do not repair generic prose by scattering “Cool,” “Let\'s,” or “That\'s it” through it. Repair the missing reasoning, the abrupt transition, or the abstract example.\\r\\n554: \\r\\n555: ### Finally, verify the student surface\\r\\n556: \\r\\n557: Check the actual prompt, answer state, labels, buttons, diagrams, and explanations. A good note can be undermined by a shell that exposes internal planning or reveals the answer too soon. Preview desktop and mobile when the interface changes. Keep the instructor\'s planning map separate from what the learner sees.\\r\\n558: \\r\\n559: ## Use this rubric before handing over LSAT writing\\r\\n560: \\r\\n561: Score each dimension from 0 to 2: **0** absent or misleading; **1** partly present but needing revision; **2** clear and usable. This is an editorial tool, not a validated learning measure. A logical error or scope violation requires revision regardless of the total.\\r\\n562: \\r\\n563: | Dimension | What a strong draft does |\\r\\n564: |---|---|\\r\\n565: | **Ordinary meaning** | Defines the idea in language the reader can understand before notation. |\\r\\n566: | **Concrete anchor** | Uses an original, small, unambiguous situation and states needed assumptions. |\\r\\n567: | **Visible reasoning** | Shows the intermediate connection instead of asserting a result. |\\r\\n568: | **Point of view** | Keeps the reader involved; uses teacher narration purposefully. |\\r\\n569: | **Cadence** | Gives complex thoughts room and short conclusions a clear place to land. |\\r\\n570: | **Vocabulary** | Keeps useful technical terms, explains them, and avoids internal jargon. |\\r\\n571: | **Questions and pauses** | Asks a genuine decision and gives an appropriate chance to answer. |\\r\\n572: | **Boundaries** | Shows what the information leaves open and why a tempting move fails. |\\r\\n573: | **Progression** | Each operation has a prerequisite, a reason to appear, and adequate practice. |\\r\\n574: | **Transfer** | Fresh questions require the taught skill, with reasoned feedback. |\\r\\n575: | **Tone and warmth** | Expects effort, supports the next action, and never mocks uncertainty. |\\r\\n576: | **Presentation** | Examples, comparisons, notation, and interactions clarify the reasoning. |\\r\\n577: \\r\\n578: Do not treat a high total as proof of an identical voice. Use the original source and Joshua\'s response to calibrate the subjective feel. His concrete corrections override a score.\\r\\n579: \\r\\n580: ## A reusable writing brief\\r\\n581: \\r\\n582: Use this when giving another writer or agent the task. Supply the actual source, lesson scope, prerequisite knowledge, and approved method along with it.\\r\\n583: \\r\\n584: ```text\\r\\n585: Write this LSAT material in Joshua\'s selected teaching voice.\\r\\n586: Read wiki/teaching-voice.md, references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md,\\r\\n587: and wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md before drafting.\\r\\n588: \\r\\n589: Keep the reader beside the teacher. Start with the ordinary meaning of\\r\\n590: the idea, then make it concrete with an original, intuitive example.\\r\\n591: Show each small inference in natural language. Ask the question a learner\\r\\n592: would actually need to answer here. When the question is practice, leave\\r\\n593: room for an attempt before revealing the reasoning.\\r\\n594: \\r\\n595: Vary sentence length by purpose. Let explanations breathe and use short\\r\\n596: sentences to settle a consequence or a limit. Use contractions naturally.\\r\\n597: Teach the technical term when there is a clear idea to attach it to.\\r\\n598: Repeat an anchor only when the repetition changes the learner\'s view.\\r\\n599: Show a tempting mistake and how to detect it when that distinction matters.\\r\\n600: \\r\\n601: Preserve Joshua\'s method, the exact logical strength, complete conditions,\\r\\n602: and the selected topic scope. Give fresh practice with explained answers.\\r\\n603: Do not copy the reference\'s examples, jokes, personal claims, or procedural\\r\\n604: counts. No em dashes, hype, invented evidence, internal stage language,\\r\\n605: or decorative catchphrases. Light humor is optional and must help the lesson.\\r\\n606: \\r\\n607: Read the result aloud, check every inference, and review the student-facing\\r\\n608: prompt and answer states. If the reader cannot hear why the next thought\\r\\n609: follows, revise the explanation before polishing the surface.\\r\\n610: ```\\r\\n611: \\r\\n612: ## Keep this reference current\\r\\n613: \\r\\n614: This file is the detailed method reference, not a second canonical brand voice. [Teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md) remains the router. [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md) remains the record of demonstrated wording failures. Add Joshua\'s later calibration to the relevant section here rather than creating competing LSAT voice documents.\\r\\n615: \\r\\n616: The project-owned `lsat-u-voice` skill reads this standard. Recap and bootcamp workflows also read it explicitly. An older vendor voice skill describes a different writer blend; it must not override this Joshua-selected standard in this project. Managed plugin caches are preserved rather than silently rewritten.\\r\\n617: \\r\\n618: The source archive is immutable. Future evidence or revised attachments should be recorded as a separate generation with provenance, not written over the original.\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a75359","wall_time_seconds":2.3146312,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3339,"output":"131:   @media print {\\r\\n132:     body { background: white; }\\r\\n133:     main { box-shadow: none; margin: 0; max-width: none; }\\r\\n134:   }\\r\\n135: </style>\\r\\n136: </head>\\r\\n137: <body>\\r\\n138: <main>\\r\\n139: <h1>Conditional Reasoning</h1>\\r\\n140: <div class=\\"source-note\\">Verbatim transcription of the attached images. Formatting has only been adjusted for readability and flow.</div>\\r\\n141: \\r\\n142: <h2>The Art of the If/Then</h2>\\r\\n143: \\r\\n144: <p>Conditional reasoning is the art of the <em>if/then</em>. If one thing happens, then another thing must happen. That’s it.</p>\\r\\n145: \\r\\n146: <p>Let’s explore our first conditional relationship:</p>\\r\\n147: \\r\\n148: <p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n149: \\r\\n150: <p>Because you’re a thinking human, you can probably guess what this means at a literal level. If I’m breathing, there’s air around. The core of the relationship is that simple. If we have one thing, we <strong>must</strong> have the other thing. Just like with the Power Players, we have to take this <strong>MUST</strong> seriously. There is no way around this relationship. If you have the first thing, you have to have the second thing.</p>\\r\\n151: \\r\\n152: <p>For all the ballyhoo about conditional reasoning, you would think it’s a lot more complicated than that. The key to keeping conditional reasoning simple through its many applications, which we will examine in detail throughout this chapter, is understanding the concept behind it. I want you to intuitively understand the relationship at the heart of conditionals. Once you have that, the verbal idiosyncrasies that separate one conditional from another will be nothing more than blips on the radar.</p>\\r\\n153: \\r\\n154: <p>Let’s start by taking our conditional example apart piece by piece.</p>\\r\\n155: \\r\\n156: <h3>The Sufficient Condition</h3>\\r\\n157: \\r\\n158: <div class=\\"callout\\">The “if” part of the conditional is called the sufficient condition.</div>\\r\\n159: \\r\\n160: <p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n161: \\r\\n162: <p>The sufficient condition is a door opener. It’s welcoming. It could occur, but it doesn’t have to. Just like an open door, someone may walk in, but they don’t have to. In the example, I might be breathing or I might not: I could be holding my breath trying to get rid of hiccups or I could be breathing. We don’t know. We just know what will happen if I <em>am</em> breathing.</p>\\r\\n163: \\r\\n164: <p><strong>If the sufficient condition is absent, you can completely ignore the conditional statement.</strong> If I’m not breathing, this conditional doesn’t matter at all. There might be air; there might not be air. The conditional wasn’t activated, so who cares? I’m not breathing, so it doesn’t matter.</p>\\r\\n165: \\r\\n166: <p><strong>But if the sufficient condition is present, the conditional statement must be followed.</strong> If I am breathing, there absolutely 100% has to be air. The conditional statement gets activated and everything goes according to plan.</p>\\r\\n167: \\r\\n168: <div class=\\"note\\">\\r\\n169:   <strong>What does it mean to activate a conditional?</strong>\\r\\n170:   <p>It means the sufficient condition is present! So the conditional must be followed.</p>\\r\\n171: </div>\\r\\n172: \\r\\n173: <p>The sufficient condition extends beyond just the word “if.” There is a whole family of words that work just like “if”; they let you know you have a sufficient condition on your hands.</p>\\r\\n174: \\r\\n175: <p>Here are the members of the sufficient family:</p>\\r\\n176: \\r\\n177: <table>\\r\\n178:   <thead><tr><th>Sufficient Indicators</th><th>Example</th></tr></thead>\\r\\n179:   <tbody>\\r\\n180:     <tr><td>if</td><td><em>If I’m breathing, then air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n181:     <tr><td>when(ever)</td><td><em>When I’m breathing, air is present.</em><br><br><em>Whenever I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n182:     <tr><td>any(time)</td><td><em>Any breathing requires that air is present.</em><br><br><em>Anytime I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n183:     <tr><td>all</td><td><em>All instances of breathing have air present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n184:     <tr><td>every(time)</td><td><em>Every instance of breathing has air present.</em><br><br><em>Everytime I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n185:     <tr><td>in order to</td><td><em>In order to breathe, I must have air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n186:     <tr><td>people who</td><td><em>People who breathe need air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n187:     <tr><td>each</td><td><em>Each instance of breathing has air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n188:   </tbody>\\r\\n189: </table>\\r\\n190: \\r\\n191: <p>Notice how all of the sufficient family members have similar personalities. They are all inclusive, open words. Think back to the similarities between these words if/when you get lost in complex language.</p>\\r\\n192: \\r\\n193: <p>I had to smooth out some of the linguistic rough edges to translate that conditional into all the different versions, but the core relationship remained the same. <strong>Language is the challenge of conditionals; you have to distill the same conditional relationship from many different wording combinations.</strong> This is why it’s so important to understand the relationship on a deeper level than “look for indicator words.” The words are the hard part, so relying on indicators exclusively is a ticking time bomb.</p>\\r\\n194: \\r\\n195: <div class=\\"note\\">\\r\\n196:   <strong>What’s with all the parentheses? What does when(ever) mean?</strong>\\r\\n197:   <p>The parentheses are referring to an optional addition to the original word. Both “when” and “whenever” are members of the sufficient family. Conditionally, all of the examples in the chart above mean exactly the same thing as our initial example.</p>\\r\\n198: </div>\\r\\n199: \\r\\n200: <h3>The Necessary Condition</h3>\\r\\n201: \\r\\n202: <div class=\\"callout\\">The “then” part of the conditional is called the necessary condition.</div>\\r\\n203: \\r\\n204: <p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n205: \\r\\n206: <p>The necessary condition slams the door that the sufficient condition opens. It is the place of certainty. I mean just look at its name! It’s called the <em>necessary</em> condition because it is literally necessary. It has to occur at some point if the conditional relationship is activated. You can prove things that live in the necessary condition.</p>\\r\\n207: \\r\\n208: <p>There is a family of words that serves the same purpose as “then.” Each of these words lets you know you have a necessary condition in play. Here are the members of the necessary family:</p>\\r\\n209: \\r\\n210: <table>\\r\\n211:   <thead><tr><th>Necessary Indicators</th><th>Example</th></tr></thead>\\r\\n212:   <tbody>\\r\\n213:     <tr><td>then</td><td><em>If I’m breathing, then air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n214:     <tr><td>must</td><td><em>Air must be present for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n215:     <tr><td>necessary</td><td><em>Air is necessary for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n216:     <tr><td>required</td><td><em>Air is required for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n217:     <tr><td>only (if)</td><td><em>Only the presence of air allows for breathing.</em><br><br><em>I’m breathing, only if air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n218:     <tr><td>depends</td><td><em>Breathing depends on air being present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n219:     <tr><td>need (to)</td><td><em>I need air to breathe.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n220:     <tr><td>have to</td><td><em>I have to have air, if I’m breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n221:     <tr><td>essential</td><td><em>Air is essential for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n222:     <tr><td>precondition</td><td><em>Air is a precondition for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n223:   </tbody>\\r\\n224: </table>\\r\\n225: \\r\\n226: <p>Notice the similarities in each of these words. They are all ironclad words. They are serious and restrictive; they are obligations.</p>\\r\\n227: \\r\\n228: <p>All of those statements in the example column mean the exact same thing as the sufficient family examples. It is the exact same concept expressed in a bunch of different ways. What’s important isn’t how the conditional is expressed, but the core relationship hiding underneath the language. This is why we have a symbolic way of representing conditional relationships, to push past the language and focus only on the relationship between the two variables.</p>\\r\\n229: \\r\\n230: <div class=\\"note\\"><strong>Pay special attention to ONLY IF!</strong> It is a necessary indicator, even though it contains the word IF. You can think of it like the ONLY is dominant over a recessive IF.</div>\\r\\n231: \\r\\n232: <hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n233: \\r\\n234: <h2>The Contrapositive</h2>\\r\\n235: \\r\\n236: <p>So far, we’ve explored the sufficient condition, the necessary condition, and the relationship between the two. Specifically, we’ve gone over the core of the conditional relationship:</p>\\r\\n237: \\r\\n238: <p class=\\"example\\">If the sufficient condition occurs, then the necessary condition must occur.</p>\\r\\n239: \\r\\n240: <p>This relationship is ironclad. We’ll call it the <strong>Must Relationship</strong>. Remember this as we explore a conditional thought experiment.</p>\\r\\n241: \\r\\n242: <p>Imagine you’re a coffee-obsessed individual. If you’re walking, you’re on your way to get a coffee. Getting a coffee is the only reason you ever walk anywhere. Every single time you walk, you are getting a coffee.</p>\\r\\n243: \\r\\n244: <p>Now, imagine that you are not on your way to get a coffee.</p>\\r\\n245: \\r\\n246: <p>What do you know about yourself?</p>\\r\\n247: \\r\\n248: <p>Put your guess here: ______________________________</p>\\r\\n249: \\r\\n250: <div class=\\"stop\\">STOP</div>\\r\\n251: \\r\\n252: <p>Really try to figure this out right now. Don’t read the solution until you have a guess. I put this question to every student. The ones that hazard a guess (either voluntarily or under protest) always come away better off.</p>\\r\\n253: \\r\\n254: <p>If you are not on your way to get a coffee, you haven’t met your necessary burden, right? So could you have activated your sufficient condition? NO.</p>\\r\\n255: \\r\\n256: <p><strong>The fact that you are not on your way to get a coffee means you are not walking.</strong></p>\\r\\n257: \\r\\n258: <div class=\\"remember\\">REMEMBER!</div>\\r\\n259: <p class=\\"example\\">If the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too. This is the contrapositive.</p>\\r\\n260: \\r\\n261: <p>As you’ve seen, the contrapositive can be produced organically. It is not some foreign process that needs to be performed on conditionals. <strong>The contrapositive is sitting there within the original conditional, waiting to be recognized.</strong> If you have not met your necessary burden, you cannot have activated the sufficient condition.</p>\\r\\n262: \\r\\n263: <p>Let’s return to our original example to see how its contrapositive looks:</p>\\r\\n264: \\r\\n265: <p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n266: \\r\\n267: <p>OK, so if I’m breathing, air is needed. I have to have air for the breathing to happen. The air is completely necessary. What if I don’t have air? That means I don’t have the thing I need. That implies that I couldn’t have activated my conditional relationship. So, I can’t be breathing. No necessary conditions means there’s no sufficient condition.</p>\\r\\n268: \\r\\n269: <p><strong>The original statement and its contrapositive are all you know from your conditional relationship.</strong> The LSAT will try to tempt you into erroneously surmising other things from your conditional. Don’t fall for these ploys. For example, they will give you a conditional statement, and then say, “We know the necessary condition is happening!” When they do this, you should be like, “Cool. So I can ignore the conditional.” <strong>The presence of the necessary condition doesn’t make anything happen.</strong></p>\\r\\n270: \\r\\n271: <p>Check out this chart showing what does and <em>does not</em> activate your conditional relationship:</p>\\r\\n272: \\r\\n273: <table>\\r\\n274:   <thead>\\r\\n275:     <tr><th>Situations</th><th>What Has to Happen?</th><th>If I’m Breathing, Then Air Is Present.</th></tr>\\r\\n276:   </thead>\\r\\n277:   <tbody>\\r\\n278:     <tr><td><strong>Sufficient Present</strong></td><td><em>Must have necessary condition</em></td><td><em>I’m breathing, so there must be air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n279:     <tr><td><strong>Necessary Absent</strong></td><td><em>Can’t have sufficient condition</em></td><td><em>There’s no air, so I can’t breathe.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n280:     <tr><td><strong>Sufficient Absent</strong></td><td><em>Nothing!</em></td><td><em>There’s no breathing happening, so we don’t know anything else.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n281:     <tr><td><strong>Necessary Present</strong></td><td><em>Nothing!</em></td><td><em>There’s air around, so we don’t know anything else.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n282:   </tbody>\\r\\n283: </table>\\r\\n284: \\r\\n285: <p>The first two rows of this chart represent the only two situations that make anything happen with your conditional relationship. The conditional is just a rule; it takes the right situation to activate it. Literally anything other than those two things can happen and you don’t need to do anything with your conditional. <strong>Without the situations in the first two rows of this chart, the conditional might as well not exist.</strong></p>\\r\\n286: \\r\\n287: <hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n288: \\r\\n289: <h2>How to Diagram Conditionals</h2>\\r\\n290: \\r\\n"}'}]

Choose the function before writing the question.\\r\\n199: \\r\\n200: | Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |\\r\\n201: |---|---|---|\\r\\n202: | **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Scope stops at Group 2. Use plain, direct, natural language, short sentences and concrete examples. Explain why each conclusion follows. No hype, no em dashes, no invented LSAT statistics, no AI phrases such as unlock, delve or game-changer. Do not introduce contrapositives, Groups 3/4, chains or quantities. Sufficient means enough; necessary means required. Arrows express a relationship, not necessarily a cause or time order. A rule does not prove that its sufficient condition occurs. All dogs are mammals permits mammals that are not dogs and also does not require sets to have different sizes. Never automatically assign words after an indicator: ask what complete idea it refers to and check the whole meaning. Current Group 1 list: if, when, where, all, the only, every, any. Current Group 2 list: only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must. These are teaching groups, not universally mechanical word rules. If and only if differ; only members are admitted and the only people admitted are members both mean admitted -> member. Include original practice only, no copied official LSAT questions. Preserve the author\'s reasoning, but improve stiff phrasing. Teach before testing.\\r\\n"}}'}]

The script fetches, merges `origin/main` if it diverged (never rebases, never force-pushes), then pushes. It also gates on `npm run typecheck`.\\n- **Never roll `main` back.** Do not use Lovable\'s \\"Revert to commit\\", \\"revert and resend\\", or history restore, and never reset or force-push `main`. Emit a 6-8 line briefing (where we left off, Now, Next, open threads, hot, suggested next) and wait. Do not deep-read other pages until pointed at a task.\\n- `wrapup` — end of session. return record;\\n}\\nexport function elapsedSeconds(run: ConditionalRun, now = Date.now()): number {\\n  return run.elapsed + (run.timerStartedAt ? Math.max(0, Math.floor((now - Date.parse(run.timerStartedAt)) / 1000)) : 0);\\n}\\nexport function recommendNext(progress: ConditionalProgress, units: ConditionalUnit[], startingUnitId?: string) {\\n  for (const unit of getCurriculumUnits(units, startingUnitId)) {\\n    const difficulty = getCurriculumDifficulty(unit);\\n    const phase = unit.phase;\\n    if (!progress.lessons[unit.id]) return { mode: \'learn\' as const, unitId: unit.id, label: `Learn: ${unit.title}`, difficulty, phase };\\n    if (!progress.attempts.some(attempt => attempt.questionId === unit.guidedExample.id)) return { mode: \'learn\' as const, unitId: unit.id, label: `Guided example: ${unit.title}`, difficulty, phase };\\n    if (unit.drills[difficulty].some(question => !progress.attempts.some(attempt => attempt.questionId === question.id && attempt.difficulty === difficulty))) {\\n      const level = { beginner: 1, medium: 2, advanced: 3 }[difficulty];\\n      return { mode: \'practice\' as const, unitId: unit.id, label: `Level ${level} activity: ${unit.title}`, difficulty, phase };\\n    }\\n  }\\n  return null;\\n}\\n\\r\\n"}}'}]

## assistant

The safe reconciliation keeps the saved order of available questions and appends new ones. That preserves the current draft and checked-answer feedback while reopening finished runs for unanswered additions. Historical review also needs the retired question’s original options so old choices keep their meaning.

## assistant

Proposed fix, pending approval:

- Reconcile saved runs in the shared QuestionRunner. Keep available saved IDs in their original order, append new IDs, preserve run identity, drafts, attempts, timing, and valid current-question feedback.
- Reopen finished old runs at unanswered current items. Retired IDs leave only the active sequence.
- Resolve retired review questions through `findLegacyConditionalQuestion`.
- Preserve a validated `questionsUpdated` flag to offer “Review expanded lesson” without clearing lesson-read records.
- Suppress the explanation paragraph only when it exactly duplicates the reasoning steps.
- Test the actual route flow for expanded finished runs, unchanged runs, active drafts, checked-feedback/Next, retired current IDs, and historical answer semantics.

No app files edited.

## assistant

I’ll implement the compatibility fix using a small content revision registry. Saved runs will change only for the registered opening-question updates, so switching assignments cannot rewrite a shared run.
